62em){.__m__-_R_2muqssm_{--col-flex-grow:auto;--col-flex-basis:58.333333333333336%;--col-max-width:58.333333333333336%;}}</style><div class="m_96bdd299 mantine-Grid-col __m__-_R_2muqssm_"><div style="--container-size:var(--container-size-xxl);max-width:calc(97.5rem * var(--mantine-scale));padding-inline:0rem" class="m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-size="xxl" data-strategy="block" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Our threat hunters have been busy searching for abuse of the recently-released zero-day remote code execution bug in Microsoft Office (<code>CVE-2022-30190</code>). As part of their investigations, they found evidence of a threat actor hosting malicious payloads on what appears to be an <code>Australian VOIP telecommunications provider</code> with a presence in the <code>South Pacific nation of Palau</code>.</p><p>Further analysis indicated that targets in <code>Palau</code> were sent malicious documents that, when opened, exploited this vulnerability, causing victim computers to contact the provider’s website, download and execute the malware, and subsequently become infected.</p><p><strong>Key Observations</strong></p><p>This threat was a complex multi-stage operation utilizing <code>LOLBAS</code> (Living off the Land Binaries And Scripts), which allowed the attacker to initialize the attack using the <code>CVE-2022-30190</code> vulnerability within the <code>Microsoft Support Diagnostic Tool</code>. This vulnerability enables threat actors to run malicious code without the user downloading an executable to their machine which might be detected by endpoint detection.</p><p>Multiple stages of this malware were signed with a legitimate company certificate to add additional legitimacy and minimize the chance of detection.</p><p><strong>First stage</strong></p><p>The compromised website, as pictured in the screenshot below, was used to host <code>robots.txt</code> which is an executable which was disguised as “robots.txt”. We believe the name was used to conceal itself from detection if found in network logs. Using the Diagnostics Troubleshooting Wizard (<code>msdt.exe</code>), this file “robots.txt” was downloaded and saved as the file (<code>Sihost.exe</code>) and then executed.</p></div><div src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2F102473DF-5366-4217-9D40-7123CFDED20A.png.webp%3Fitok%3D24HZ5fkS&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Second Stage, Sihost.exe</strong></p><p>When the renamed “robots.txt” – “Sihost.exe” – was executed by msdt.exe it downloaded the second stage of the attack which was a loader with the hash <code>b63fbf80351b3480c62a6a5158334ec8e91fecd057f6c19e4b4dd3febaa9d447</code>. This executable was then used to download and decrypt the third stage of the attack, an encrypted file stored as ‘<code>favicon.svg</code>’ on the same web server.</p><p><strong>Third stage, favicon.svg</strong></p><p>After this file has been decrypted, it is used to download the fourth stage of the attack from <code>palau.voipstelecom.com[.]au.</code> These files are named <code>Sevntx64.exe</code> and <code>Sevntx.lnk</code>, which are then executed on the victims’ machine.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" malware family; <code>Sevntx64.exe</code> is signed with the same compromised certificate as seen previously in “robots.txt”.</p><p>The screenshot below shows the executable loading the shellcode.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace we published the first part of this research, we said the investigation was still ongoing. That was not a closing line. We were already looking at the infrastructure, the signals behind the attacks, and the scale of the reservation data abuse we were seeing in our telemetry.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThen something else happened.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFriends, colleagues, and other contacts started forwarding us messages they had received from Booking.com. The messages warned that unauthorized third parties may have accessed information linked to their reservations. Shortly after, media outlets began reporting the same thing: Booking.com had notified affected customers, reset reservation PINs, and said the accessed information could include names, email addresses, phone numbers, booking details, and information shared with accommodations. Booking.com said financial information was not accessed from its systems.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe timing mattered, but not because it started our investigation. It mattered because it publicly confirmed the kind of reservation-data exposure our first article had described from the attack side.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://us.norton.com/blog/online-scams/reservation-hijacking-scam\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eReservation Hijack scams\u003c/a\u003e work because criminals do not have to guess. A traveler receives a message about a real booking. The message contains details that make sense: the right accommodation, the right trip context, sometimes the right contact information. The link then opens a fraudulent page built around the same accommodation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA generic \u003ca href=\"https://us.norton.com/blog/online-scams/what-is-phishing \" target=\"_blank\"\u003ephishing message\u003c/a\u003e has to create trust from nothing. This scam steps into a relationship that already exists between the traveler, the booking platform, and the accommodation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhile those public warnings were appearing, Gen Threat Labs continued canvassing the data we had available: blocked URLs, fraudulent landing pages, SMS messages, emails, WhatsApp messages, in-app message evidence, and other signals connected to attacks we had already stopped. We used AI-assisted analysis to connect pieces that would otherwise remain scattered: messages, landing pages, accommodation names, URLs, templates, and infrastructure patterns. Human analysts reviewed the results and separated what we could prove from what we could only suspect.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe result is a much clearer picture.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe identified more than \u003cstrong\u003e350 compromised accommodations\u003c/strong\u003e linked to the Reservation Hijack scam flows, spread across \u003cstrong\u003e50 countries\u003c/strong\u003e. These were not abstract indicators or generic travel-themed pages. In the cases we analyzed, victims had already received reservation-specific information before being pushed to a fraudulent page customized around the accommodation tied to that booking.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is the difference between ordinary travel phishing and this model. The attackers are not guessing who is traveling. They already have enough reservation context to make the fraud feel normal.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd they are doing it at scale.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-05/4.png.webp?itok=--3yMOGq 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-05/4.png.webp?itok=XvdxJ47c 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":936,"height":936},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-05/4.png.webp?itok=dssEfnxL 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-05/4.png.webp?itok=echDsf-v 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":890},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-05/4.png.webp?itok=QpL1_kaz 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-05/4.png.webp?itok=rzJ7P53H 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":690}],"caption":"Key figures from Gen Threat Labs’ analysis of compromised accommodations linked to intercepted Reservation Hijack attacks.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-05/4.png.webp?itok=QpL1_kaz","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch3\u003eHow we connected the evidence\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe second phase of our investigation was already underway when Booking.com’s customer notifications started circulating publicly.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eGen’s view of this activity comes from attacks we blocked before they could complete. No single signal tells the whole story. An SMS or WhatsApp message may show the \u003ca href=\"https://us.norton.com/blog/emerging-threats/what-is-social-engineering \" target=\"_blank\"\u003esocial engineering\u003c/a\u003e. A URL may show the redirect path. A landing page may show the accommodation being impersonated. An in-app message may show where the attacker entered the trusted communication flow. A repeated page structure may link incidents that look separate at first.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePut together, those pieces show how reservation data moves from compromise to fraud.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAI helped us work through that evidence at scale. We used it to normalize accommodation names, cluster similar messages, compare landing-page structures, connect URLs with page content, and flag repeated patterns across channels. This was not “AI says these attacks are connected.” It was AI-assisted triage followed by human review.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom that process, we extracted and cleaned accommodation names from captured fraudulent landing pages and identified \u003cstrong\u003e350 compromised accommodations\u003c/strong\u003e.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe use “compromised accommodations” carefully here. It means reservation context tied to those accommodations was compromised and used in an attack flow we intercepted. The source of compromise may vary: a hotel account, a booking platform partner account, an email inbox, a property management system, a guest messaging workflow, or a third-party service provider.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe end result for the traveler is the same. Criminals had access to information that should have stayed inside the booking relationship.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is also a conservative number. It only includes attacks where we had enough evidence to connect the flow to a specific accommodation. It does not include people who received a message and never clicked, attacks outside our telemetry, landing pages we did not capture, or cases where the accommodation name could not be reliably extracted.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSo 350 is not the size of the problem. It is the part we could see clearly.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eFrom scam pattern to repeatable operation\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eA single compromised hotel account can explain a few victims. A copied phishing kit can explain a short campaign. A traveler may book a large hotel chain, a family-run guesthouse, a serviced apartment, a hostel, or a villa through similar platforms and messaging flows.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom the traveler’s point of view, the trust model is almost the same. They made a booking. They expect communication. They may not know whether the message came from the property, the platform, a channel manager, a property management system, or a compromised account sitting somewhere in between.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is exactly the gap criminals are abusing.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eHow big could this problem be?\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo understand the potential scale of Reservation Hijack scams, we looked at the accommodation properties appearing in our research dataset.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn total, we identified \u003cstrong\u003e350 accommodation entries\u003c/strong\u003e linked to reservation hijack scam activity. These were not all the same kind of business. The dataset included hotels, aparthotels, resorts, hostels, guesthouses, villas, apartments and other accommodation types. For each one, we estimated the number of rooms, units, beds or capsules, depending on the property type, and used that to calculate the likely maximum guest capacity.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAcross the deduplicated dataset, these properties represented an estimated maximum capacity of around \u003cstrong\u003e82,000 guests at any given time\u003c/strong\u003e. Using a conservative occupancy rate of \u003cstrong\u003e50%\u003c/strong\u003e and an average length of stay of \u003cstrong\u003e2.5 nights\u003c/strong\u003e, this translates into approximately \u003cstrong\u003e6 million guest stays per year\u003c/strong\u003e.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat does not mean 6 million people were scammed. It does not mean 6 million people had their data exposed. What it shows is the size of the opportunity criminals may be looking at when they compromise accommodation or booking communication channels.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA single compromised workflow can give scammers something much more valuable than a random phone number or email address. It can give them context: the name of the accommodation, the dates of the stay, the booking amount, the channel the guest expects to be contacted through, and the right moment to create urgency.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is why Reservation Hijack scams are so convincing. The scam does not feel like spam. Many pages used the same internal paths for scripts, images and styles. Several included the same unusual overlay component, repeated internal asset paths, and the same card-validation language, including claims that funds would be reserved and refunded within 10 minutes.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe pages were also built to look specific to the victim. Hotel names appeared in page titles and visible content. Prices varied from one case to another. Check-in and check-out dates were inserted into the page. In some cases, page metadata and images were synchronized with the accommodation being impersonated. The evidence points to a repeatable process for turning reservation context into property-specific phishing pages. Taken together, those patterns are consistent with a phishing kit designed to generate accommodation-specific pages at scale, rather than pages built manually for each victim.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe phishing pages also included a fake live support chat. To the victim, it looked like part of the booking or payment process, but it was controlled from the phishing infrastructure. The payment step could therefore become a live interaction: someone behind the page could react to the victim’s input, ask for another card, explain away a failed “validation”, push the victim to continue, or try to capture a one-time code while the victim still believed they were talking to booking support.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSome of the infrastructure also uses legitimate internet services, including services such as Cloudflare, to place a layer between the visible scam page and the systems behind it. That does not make those services malicious. It means criminals are abusing the same infrastructure that normal websites use, including protective layers that can obscure hosting details, traffic paths, and backend systems.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor investigators, this kind of setup makes the infrastructure harder to trace. For travelers, it removes many of the usual warning signs. They see a page that loads normally, shows the right accommodation, includes a support chat, and refers to the trip they actually booked. At that point, many people will treat the request as part of the reservation process, even when it is not.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eCorrect information is not proof of legitimacy\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor years, people have been told to watch for bad spelling, odd sender names, generic greetings, and suspicious links. That advice still helps, but it does not solve this problem.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA message can include the right accommodation, the right destination, and details from a real stay, and still be malicious. A page can show the correct hotel name, dates, and a plausible payment amount, and still be fake. A request can arrive at the moment when a traveler expects a check-in or payment message, and still be fraud.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePage behavior can create false confidence too. In some of the phishing pages we analyzed, the fake support chat looked like part of the booking or payment process. If the victim had a question, or if the card “validation” failed, the page could keep the interaction going instead of simply collecting data and stopping there.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is what makes Reservation Hijack scams difficult for travelers to judge. The victim is not being tricked only by a fake brand or a fake website. They are being pushed through a process built around real context, at the moment when that context feels normal.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTravelers expect hotels and booking platforms to contact them. They expect last-minute requests, check-in instructions, payment checks, ID forms, and confirmation messages. Criminals are not fighting that expectation. They are using it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWhat travelers should do\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe safest rule is simple: \u003cstrong\u003edo not treat correct booking information as proof that a message is legitimate.\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf a message asks you to verify payment details, confirm a reservation, re-enter card information, pay a balance, reset a PIN, or avoid cancellation, do not use the \u003ca href=\"https://us.norton.com/blog/online-scams/i-clicked-on-a-phishing-link\" target=\"_blank\"\u003elink in the message\u003c/a\u003e.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOpen the booking platform or hotel app directly. Go to the official website yourself. Use contact details from the original booking confirmation. If needed, call the property using a verified number. Check whether the same request appears inside your official booking account.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis applies even if the message mentions the right hotel, the right destination, or details from your stay.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBooking.com has told customers that it will not ask guests to share credit card details by email, phone, WhatsApp, or text, or ask for a bank transfer that differs from the payment policy in the booking confirmation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you already entered payment details into a suspicious page, contact your bank immediately, freeze or cancel the card, and monitor for follow-on fraud. Criminals may reuse the card data later, or sell it to others.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWhat accommodations should take from this\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor hotels and other accommodation providers, this is not only a guest-awareness issue. Guest communication has become part of the attack surface.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe systems and workflows between the property and the guest are now valuable targets. That can include hotel staff accounts, booking platform partner accounts, email inboxes, property management systems, guest messaging tools, channel managers, and third-party service providers.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe lures we have seen on the accommodation side are not exotic. They look like normal hotel work. Some impersonate Booking.com and claim there is a guest complaint waiting for a response. Others say that a paid reservation needs urgent verification, or that the property’s visibility or ranking may be affected if staff do not act quickly. We have also seen payment and invoice-themed messages designed to make staff open attachments or documents that can lead to credential theft or \u003ca href=\"https://us.norton.com/blog/malware/signs-of-malware\" target=\"_blank\"\u003emalware infection\u003c/a\u003e.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-05/imag6.png.webp?itok=i8Ee2qDm 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-05/imag6.png.webp?itok=3EzsIFYx 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":688,"height":1286},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-05/imag6.png.webp?itok=bsSzWuDp 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-05/imag6.png.webp?itok=nkkDo86h 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":688,"height":1286},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-05/imag6.png.webp?itok=mCTAd9q8 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-05/imag6.png.webp?itok=5XsDqvxH 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":688,"height":1286}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-05/imag6.png.webp?itok=mCTAd9q8","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThat is why the first visible sign of a Reservation Hijack scam may not be the message sent to the guest. It may start earlier, with an email to a shared hotel inbox, a fake partner-support notice, a malicious attachment, or a document that looks like part of normal accounting or reservation handling. Once reservation context is obtained, whether directly or through another actor in the criminal ecosystem, it can be used later to make guest-facing scams much more convincing.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor accommodation providers, staff training needs to cover the workflows criminals are actually abusing. Urgent complaints, reservation-verification requests, payment advice emails, invoice notifications, security-update prompts, and any message asking staff to open an attachment, enable content, sign in again, or act under time pressure should all be treated as possible entry points into the same fraud chain.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe defensive work starts with account protection, but it should not stop there. Accommodation providers need strong \u003ca href=\"https://us.norton.com/blog/account-safety/multi-factor-authentication\" target=\"_blank\"\u003emulti-factor authentication\u003c/a\u003e, tight control over who can access guest data, monitoring for unusual logins or message patterns, and a clear process for responding when an account may have been abused. The devices used to manage reservations, guest messages, invoices and payment-related emails should also be protected with reliable security protection. Some of the lures we observed were designed to make staff open attachments or documents that could install malware or steal credentials. In those cases, \u003ca href=\"https://us.norton.com/antivirus\" target=\"_blank\"\u003eantivirus\u003c/a\u003e or endpoint protection may be the last barrier before a compromised inbox becomes a compromised booking workflow.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePayment-related communication deserves special attention. If a guest receives a payment or verification request, there should be a reliable way to confirm whether it is real. If an account is suspected of being compromised, guests need to be warned quickly and clearly.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe reputational damage can be painful. Even when the property is also a victim, the traveler may remember the incident as “the hotel message that stole my card details.” That makes workflow security part of customer trust.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWhat the wider travel ecosystem should learn\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eReservation Hijack scams sit between several parts of the travel industry. The booking platform may see unusual partner activity. The hotel may see confused guests. The traveler sees a convincing message. The bank sees card fraud. A security company sees the landing page.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach view is partial, and that delay helps the scam continue.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe useful signals are often scattered: unusual partner logins, repeated payment-verification language, guest-message spikes, reused templates, fake landing pages, redirects, and infrastructure overlaps. Sharing those signals faster would make it easier to see when separate complaints are really part of the same operation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOne compromised accommodation can look like a local incident. Hundreds of compromised accommodations across 50 countries point to a repeatable abuse model.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eThe next phase of travel fraud\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eReservation Hijack scams exploit a simple mistake in how we judge trust online.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTravelers think: “This message knows my booking, so it must be real.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCriminals know: “If we have the booking context, the victim will do much of the trust work for us.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe message does not have to be perfect. The website does not have to be flawless. The attacker does not need to convince the victim from zero. They only need to appear inside a relationship that already exists.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur latest data shows that this model has spread well beyond isolated cases. We identified \u003cstrong\u003e350 compromised accommodations across 50 countries\u003c/strong\u003e, covering hotels, apartments, hostels, resorts, guesthouses, villas, and other accommodation types. 2026, attackers compromised the official JDownloader website and swapped download links to serve trojanized installers. The AV service is effectively dead. It won't run, it can't scan, and Windows Update can't push signature updates. One victim in the Reddit thread reported that Windows Defender disappeared entirely after running the trojanized installer.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is a known but uncommon TTP (MITRE T1562.001). Users who clicked through both warnings — SmartScreen and UAC — gave the malware everything it needed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow the dropper provisions the bot\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eStage 2's strings are all XOR-obfuscated with the same \u003ccode\u003efywo\u003c/code\u003e key used for its resources. We recovered two configuration values:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e15e9100cccadef17d91598e244b0b038\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCampaignID\u003c/strong\u003e a unique identifier for this campaign, used during bot registration with the C2 server.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e7a2666057614636498fba93e483028cf\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eDDRList\u003c/strong\u003e six dead-drop resolver URLs that the bot uses to discover its C2 servers (detailed in the next section).\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eNotably, the dropper does not write a C2 server list directly. The bot discovers its command-and-control addresses exclusively through the dead-drop resolver mechanism — a design choice that makes the infrastructure resilient to takedowns.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eDead-drop resolvers: how the bot finds its C2\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eA dead-drop resolver (DDR) is an intermediary, a legitimate web page where the operator publishes encrypted C2 addresses. The bot fetches the page, extracts and decrypts the content, and connects to whatever C2 servers it finds. If the operator needs to rotate infrastructure, they update the DDR page and every infected machine follows automatically.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe bot reads its DDR list from the registry, fetches each URL, and RC4-decrypts the page content. Valid payloads start with the prefix \u003ccode\u003eVAL:\u003c/code\u003e followed by a semicolon-separated list of C2 URLs.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe extracted all six dead-drop resolver URLs from the dropper and probed them:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003eURL\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eStatus\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eDecrypted content\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003etelegra[.]ph/tr02-05-02\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eC2 list: parkspringshotel[.]com + auraguest[.]lk\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003etelegra[.]ph/ki06-05-02\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003eVAL:XX\u003c/code\u003e — placeholder for future C2 rotation\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003erentry[.]co/zzk7opdu\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eSame C2 list\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003erentry[.]org/wayc2qqz\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLive\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003eVAL:XX\u003c/code\u003e — placeholder\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003ecodeberg[.]org/etienne74/guix/...\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e404\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eRemoved or never created\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e`2wxjoz6lkdxkniksujbdknkugweemx7uvxdr4ejgyipce4tnvktvbvqd.onion/t`\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eNot probed\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eTor hidden service DDR\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe operator has two live C2 servers and four reserve dead-drop pages — two stubs ready for C2 rotation and one Tor fallback. If the current C2 infrastructure gets burned, the operator updates a Telegraph or Rentry page and every infected machine picks up the new address on its next poll cycle.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBeyond the dead-drop mechanism, the bot has a domain generation algorithm (DGA) as a last-resort fallback. It combines four seeds (\u003ccode\u003eXNerWs4I\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003ee55gegnW\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eRjWN0Y9m\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eAemOU1co\u003c/code\u003e) with 14 TLDs and an ISO-week-based rotation to produce 56 candidate domains per week. We probed all current DGA domains — none are registered. The operator is running purely on dead-drop infrastructure for now, but the DGA ensures the botnet can survive even if all resolver pages and C2 servers are taken down simultaneously.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe C2 protocol: RSA-OAEP wrapping AES-GCM\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe reversed the bot's full communication protocol from the PyArmor-protected bytecode. The server proves possession of the private key by returning \u003ccode\u003e\"ok\"\u003c/code\u003e encrypted with AES-GCM. From that point, every message in both directions is AES-256-GCM encrypted with a per-direction sequence counter as authenticated associated data — preventing replay and reordering.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter the handshake, the bot registers with the campaign ID provisioned by the dropper. The server returns a \u003ccode\u003ebot_id\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003ebot_pass\u003c/code\u003e that the bot stores in the registry for subsequent polling. The bot's primary capability is arbitrary Python code execution — the attacker can ship any Python code and the bot will run it and return the output.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAll identified C2 and staging servers are compromised \u003ca href=\"https://qloapps.com/\"\u003eQloApps\u003c/a\u003e hotel reservation websites:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003eDomain\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eRole\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eIP\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003ePHP\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eLocation\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003eparkspringshotel[.]com\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eC2 panel\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e172.96.172[.]91\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e8.3.30\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eGhana\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003eauraguest[.]lk\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eC2 panel\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e209.133.215[.]178\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e8.3.30\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eSri Lanka\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003ccode\u003echeckinnhotels[.]com\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eLinux payload staging\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e66.29.137[.]25\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e7.4.33\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e—\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe two C2 servers share identical JARM fingerprints and run the same PHP version, confirming the same C2 panel software is deployed on both. The third domain, \u003ccode\u003echeckinnhotels[.]com\u003c/code\u003e, was used to host the Linux payload disguised as an SVG file (\u003ca href=\"https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/jdownloader-site-hacked-to-replace-installers-with-python-rat-malware/\"\u003eBleepingComputer\u003c/a\u003e). All three run QloApps — an open-source hotel booking CMS — suggesting the attacker specifically targets this software to build their infrastructure. None of the domains had malicious detections on VirusTotal at the time of discovery. We confirmed both C2 servers remain operationally live as of May 11.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe r77 rootkit stager\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe rootkit stager is a 176 KB native x86 PE based on the open-source \u003ca href=\"https://github.com/bytecode77/r77-rootkit\"\u003er77 rootkit\u003c/a\u003e. It contains an embedded .NET assembly that performs four operations:\u003c/p\u003e\u003col\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e9ca3772c2bd6052d86931268e23d036a\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAMSI bypass\u003c/strong\u003e — a PowerShell cradle patches \u003ccode\u003eAmsiScanBuffer\u003c/code\u003e in memory, disabling Windows' Antimalware Scan Interface before any script payload runs.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e01c27cd4c721b2dabe18b819d05c84fe\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eReflective DLL injection\u003c/strong\u003e — injects the r77 hooking DLLs (32-bit and 64-bit variants) into \u003ccode\u003ewinlogon.exe\u003c/code\u003e. These DLLs hook Windows API functions to hide any process, file, or registry key whose name starts with the \u003ccode\u003e$77\u003c/code\u003e prefix.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e254d7da7ebd6dfd7d57f7c5f67760ba3\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eRegistry persistence\u003c/strong\u003e — stores the stager assembly as a binary blob in \u003ccode\u003eHKLM\\SOFTWARE\\$77stager\u003c/code\u003e and reloads it at every boot via .NET reflection.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ef200c38559748bd535edaf6a0d5aa3d5\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eService creation\u003c/strong\u003e — installs a Windows service (\u003ccode\u003e$77svc\u003c/code\u003e) to ensure the rootkit survives reboots independently of the registry mechanism.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003eOnce r77 is active, the \u003ccode\u003e$77\u003c/code\u003e prefix acts as an invisibility cloak. The Python bot calls \u003ccode\u003er77_hide_self()\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003er77_hide_registry_path()\u003c/code\u003e to rename its own process and registry hive with this prefix, making them invisible to Task Manager, \u003ccode\u003eregedit\u003c/code\u003e, and most security tools.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe Python bot under PyArmor v9\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe final payload is a Python script protected by \u003ca href=\"https://pyarmor.dashingsoft.com/\"\u003ePyArmor\u003c/a\u003e v9, a commercial bytecode obfuscation tool. PyArmor encrypts each function's bytecode individually and decrypts it at runtime through a native DLL — making static analysis significantly harder than standard Python malware.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe built a custom tool to decrypt and disassemble the protected bytecode, recovering 30 functions totaling 7,326 lines of disassembly. The bot is cross-platform (Windows and Linux), uses gevent for asynchronous I/O, and handles the full lifecycle: configuration management, C2 discovery via dead-drop resolvers and DGA, encrypted communication, task execution, and rootkit integration.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe bot's primary capability is executing arbitrary Python code received from the C2 server. Combined with the Python 3.14 runtime installed by the dropper, this gives the operator the ability to deploy any follow-on payload — keyloggers, credential stealers, lateral movement tools — without needing to push new binaries to the victim.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch2\u003eImpact and recommendations\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eUsers who downloaded JDownloader from the official website between May 6 and May 7, 2026, using the \"Download Alternative Installer\" links (Windows) or the Linux shell installer link are potentially compromised. The \u003ca href=\"https://jdownloader.org/incident_8.5.2026.html\"\u003eofficial incident report\u003c/a\u003e lists eight malicious installer hashes across x86/amd64 and multiple JRE versions. The attackers exploited an unpatched CMS vulnerability to swap download links — the actual installer files hosted externally were never modified. In-app updates, macOS downloads, and package manager installs (Flatpak, Winget, Snap) were not affected.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Linux attack chain, [first reported by BleepingComputer](https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/jdownloader-site-hacked-to-replace-installers-with-python-rat-malware/), follows a different delivery path. We downloaded the trojanized shell installer and its payload to verify the claims and dig deeper.d. The modified install4j script requires root, checks for x86_64 architecture, and downloads a 20 MB tar.gz from \u003ccode\u003echeckinnhotels[.]com/img/logo1.svg\u003c/code\u003e (disguised with \u003ccode\u003eContent-Type: image/svg+xml\u003c/code\u003e — still live as of May 11). The archive contains two files: a 3 KB SUID-root helper (\u003ccode\u003esystemd-exec\u003c/code\u003e) and a 21 MB PyInstaller bundle (\u003ccode\u003epkg\u003c/code\u003e). The helper is a minimal ELF that calls \u003ccode\u003esetuid(0)\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003esetgid(0)\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003eexecvp()\u003c/code\u003e — installed to \u003ccode\u003e/usr/bin/systemd-exec\u003c/code\u003e with \u003ccode\u003echmod 4755\u003c/code\u003e. Persistence is created via \u003ccode\u003e/etc/profile.d/systemd.sh\u003c/code\u003e, which launches the payload masquerading as \u003ccode\u003e/usr/libexec/upowerd\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe decrypted the PyArmor-protected Python bytecode from the Linux payload and confirmed it is \u003cstrong\u003ethe same bot source code\u003c/strong\u003e as the Windows variant — identical RSA public key, DGA seeds, RC4 keys, C2 protocol, and DDR handler logic. The same campaign ID (\u003ccode\u003eNdcHsJPM\u003c/code\u003e) and all six DDR URLs are written to \u003ccode\u003e~/.local/share/\u003c/code\u003e as RC4-encrypted files (mirroring the Windows registry approach). The only difference is the Python runtime: the Linux bundle uses Python 3.12 (packaged via PyInstaller) versus Python 3.14 on Windows.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe JDownloader developers restored clean downloads on May 9 and published a detailed incident timeline. Users who already had JDownloader installed and received updates through the application's built-in updater were not affected — only fresh downloads from the website during the compromise window were trojanized.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eIf you ran the trojanized installer:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e78c8637f87f47adb2a6c0e9e37ff1466\"\u003eThe WDAC policy may have disabled your antivirus. Check \u003ccode\u003e%WINDIR%\\System32\\CodeIntegrity\\SIPolicy.p7b\u003c/code\u003e — if it exists and you didn't create it, delete it and reboot.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e5a9344602452a9e77dfe81e30c45fd8c\"\u003eThe registry keys \u003ccode\u003eHKCU\\SOFTWARE\\Python\u003c/code\u003e (bot config) and \u003ccode\u003eHKLM\\SOFTWARE\\$77stager\u003c/code\u003e (rootkit) are strong indicators of compromise — but if r77 is active, its API hooks hide all \u003ccode\u003e$77\u003c/code\u003e-prefixed registry keys and the bot's own hive from standard tools like \u003ccode\u003eregedit\u003c/code\u003e. To inspect these, boot from a Windows recovery environment or mount the registry hives offline.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"edcbc2f271c7b2885893f8f995fe252eb\"\u003eA clean OS reinstall is the safest remediation. The r77 rootkit's reflective injection into \u003ccode\u003ewinlogon.exe\u003c/code\u003e and AMSI bypass make partial cleanup unreliable.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eFor defenders:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"efcd7ce434d65f159da0dbff097d5fb44\"\u003eThe bot uses the default Python User-Agent \u003ccode\u003ePython-urllib/3.14\u003c/code\u003e. While not unique on its own, it can help correlate connections to the C2 URLs and DDR pages listed below.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e35fe4757c01a79131defbd71e6762bfb\"\u003eMonitor for DNS queries to the DGA pattern: 15-character lowercase alphanumeric hostnames on TLDs including \u003ccode\u003e.top\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003e.cfd\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003e.sbs\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003e.cyou\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003e.icu\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003e.casa\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e41ab2df0dd210b9e338012701bdf4398\"\u003eThe mutex name \u003ccode\u003eGlobal\\0C3C1D37\u003c/code\u003e can serve as a host-based indicator.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e68e99d7961368cd02568989f8e767d08\"\u003eBlock the dead-drop resolver pages: \u003ccode\u003etelegra[.]ph/tr02-05-02\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003etelegra[.]ph/ki06-05-02\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003erentry[.]co/zzk7opdu\u003c/code\u003e, Registry\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eT1112\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor customers using Norton, Avast, AVG, Avira or any other security product, the most important question is whether the malicious installer was executed. If you downloaded an affected installer but did not run it, delete the file, download a fresh installer from the official JDownloader site, and run a full scan with up-to-date protection. If you ran the installer, or cannot rule out that you ran it, treat the system as compromised. JDownloader recommends a clean operating system reinstall in this situation, and we agree that this is the safest baseline. The Windows payload includes rootkit behavior, persistence, encrypted configuration and policy-based defense evasion, so a normal scan may reduce risk but cannot guarantee that every persistence mechanism has been removed. Until the system has been rebuilt or inspected from a trusted recovery environment, avoid using it for sensitive activity. Do not log in to email, banking, work accounts, password managers or other important services from that machine. Malware Authors Study Algebra: The Group Theory Inside Bedep's DGA","created":"1776878106","subtitle":"A closer look at how Bedep used foreign exchange data and advanced math to generate hard-to-predict domains, making its command-and-control infrastructure more difficult for defenders to block and disrupt","authors":[{"socialLinks":[],"bio":"A group of elite researchers who like to stay under the radar.","displayName":"Threat Research Team","title":"Threat Research Team","picture":{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_large/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=Y7ujGs-y 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_large_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=zua2szJm 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":425,"height":425},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_small/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=qctXxwnL 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_small_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=KgS0xjxS 2x","media":"(min-width: 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_avatar_small_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=iDSFMEuk 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":120,"height":120}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_avatar_small/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=uIL3f30w"}}],"contributingAuthors":[],"products":[],"paragraphs":[{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eTLDR: \u003c/strong\u003eBedep was a malware family active mainly in 2014 and 2015 that used an unusually sophisticated domain generation algorithm, or DGA, to hide its command-and-control infrastructure. Instead of relying only on the date, it used real foreign exchange rates published by the \u003cem\u003eEuropean Central Bank\u003c/em\u003e, making its future domains much harder to predict in advance. Under the hood, the malware used ideas from group theory to generate a fixed set of unique domains without collisions, a rare example of advanced mathematics being applied directly in real-world malware.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost domain generation algorithms are boring. A linear congruential generator, a date seed, some modular arithmetic, a character table -- \u003ccode\u003eseed = (seed * 0x41C64E6D + 0x3039) % 2^31\u003c/code\u003e, rinse, repeat. You reverse them in an afternoon, pre-compute next month's domains over lunch, and move on.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBedep author implemented a number theory textbook inside a 32-bit DLL, and the result is one of the most mathematically elegant pieces of malware ever shipped.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis post is a deep dive into this unique engineering.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat is Bedep?\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eBedep is an ad-fraud botnet that was active from late 2014 through 2015, delivered exclusively through the Angler exploit kit. When a Flash zero-day (CVE-2015-0311) was burning, Angler was dropping Bedep. The malware itself was a modular downloader focused on click fraud, but its infrastructure is what made it interesting. In early 2015, new variants appeared with a DGA for C2 resolution. A three-day sinkhole by Arbor Networks (ASERT) caught phone-homes from roughly 82,000 unique IPs, spread across every continent except -- notably -- Russia.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDennis Schwarz originally reversed the DGA at ASERT, who published a proof-of-concept Python reimplementation and a detailed write-up titled \u003cem\u003e\"Bedep's DGA: Trading Foreign Exchange for Malware Domains.\"\u003c/em\u003e The original blog post has since been taken down from NETSCOUT's site, but survives on the \u003ca href=\"https://web.archive.org/web/20160311083109/http://www.arbornetworks.com:80/blog/asert/bedeps-dga-trading-foreign-exchange-for-malware-domains/\"\u003eWayback Machine\u003c/a\u003e. The PoC lived at \u003ccode\u003egithub.com/arbor/bedep_dga\u003c/code\u003e. Dennis got the algorithm working but noted that parts of it were opaque -- he called the core transform a \"blackbox\" and flagged the embedded table as \"likely something Fermat number related.\" He was right. Let's open that box.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe ECB trick: Seeding a DGA from the financial markets\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eBefore the math starts, Bedep needs a seed that both the bot and the botmaster can independently derive without communicating. Most DGAs use the date. Bedep uses the date \u003cem\u003eand\u003c/em\u003e the global foreign exchange market.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe algorithm fetches two XML files from legitimate public services:\u003c/p\u003e\u003col\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec5890edf5187711796f804fae7ad8577\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eUTC time\u003c/strong\u003e from \u003ccode\u003eearthtools.org/timezone/0/0\u003c/code\u003e -- the current timestamp, converted to \"days since year zero.\"\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ead6313dc967de96442575ff50339b412\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eEuro foreign exchange reference rates\u003c/strong\u003e from \u003ccode\u003ewww.ecb.europa.eu/stats/eurofxref/eurofxref-hist-90d.xml\u003c/code\u003e -- the European Central Bank's daily publication of EUR conversion rates against 31+ world currencies, updated every business day.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe time is used to select which day's rates to use. Specifically, Bedep picks \"last Tuesday's\" exchange rates -- meaning the preceding week's Tuesday until Thursday, after which it rolls forward to the current week's Tuesday. Only rates falling on a Monday boundary (in the algorithm's day-counting scheme) are considered. This gives a roughly weekly rotation with a deterministic selection rule.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom the chosen date, Bedep extracts up to 48 currency rates -- USD, JPY, GBP, CZK, BGN, HUF, and so on. Each rate is parsed through a slightly broken custom \u003ccode\u003eatof()\u003c/code\u003e implementation (off by a bit in the least significant digits -- a quirk, not a feature), packed into a 64-bit IEEE double, and its low 32-bit dword is extracted for use in the algorithm.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHere is what a single run looks like, from the PoC output for April 7, 2015:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eparsed 31 currencies from 2015-04-07 (currency date): \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;USD: 1.0847 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;JPY: 130.33 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; BGN: 1.9558 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;CZK: 27.455 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;DKK: 7.4714 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;GBP: 0.7286 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;HUF: 299.08 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;PLN: 4.0578 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;RON: 4.4165 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;SEK: 9.374 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; CHF: 1.0438 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;NOK: 8.73 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;HRK: 7.619 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; RUB: 59.8265 \u0026nbsp; TRY: 2.8079 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;AUD: 1.4192 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;BRL: 3.3979 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;CAD: 1.3563 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;CNY: 6.7241 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;HKD: 8.4086 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;IDR: 14091.77 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;ILS: 4.267 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; INR: 67.598 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;KRW: 1183.28 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;MXN: 16.1919 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; MYR: 3.9527 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;NZD: 1.4423 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;PHP: 48.3 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;SGD: 1.4724 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;THB: 35.335 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;ZAR: 12.8345\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eWhy is this clever? Because the ECB rates are:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e5f3c952abb440c715bcfa626ac9bd4c0\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePublicly available\u003c/strong\u003e -- anyone can fetch the same XML\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e7730a0d72c71be00a0c3d3feebc10618\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eGlobally consistent\u003c/strong\u003e -- every bot on every continent gets the same data\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e1667d4c4edd48ffbda05118e24153bf0\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eUnpredictable in advance\u003c/strong\u003e -- nobody can predict next Tuesday's EUR/USD rate\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e80b9125dab13aae37cb9ed8b88656475\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHistorically verifiable\u003c/strong\u003e -- the ECB publishes archives going back years\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e0d9bf0abba3c6cb7703705ce6203f707\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eImpossible to tamper with\u003c/strong\u003e -- the ECB is not going to modify their reference rates to help you sinkhole a botnet\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd because the malware fetches this from \u003ccode\u003eecb.europa.eu\u003c/code\u003e, the request looks like legitimate traffic. A Snort signature (SID 33188) was written to detect it, but it fired constantly on real users checking exchange rates.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe mathematical core\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is where Bedep stops being a clever botnet and starts being a discrete mathematics exercise.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003ePrecomputed subgroup parameters\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe DLL embeds a lookup table -- 102 entries of 8 dwords each, stored as JSON in the PoC (\u003ccode\u003etransform2_table_varN.json\u003c/code\u003e, one per config variant). At first glance it looks like random constants. It is not.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe \u003ccode\u003etransform2\u003c/code\u003e function takes a hash derived from the exchange rates and days-since value, then uses it as an index into this table. Each table entry, after being decoded through config-specific XOR and multiply constants (\u003ccode\u003e0x663d81 * entry[0] ^ value3\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003e0x281 * entry[1] ^ value2\u003c/code\u003e), yields two critical values:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ee4084ac1a9ee922a9a2585119d02acb9\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ep\u003c/strong\u003e -- a prime number\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e7d298adce011a8fe31f7d5db7f1685ee\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eq\u003c/strong\u003e -- an integer such that q divides p - 1\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is the subgroup structure. The table stores primes whose group orders (p - 1) have known factorizations with small prime factors. The malware doesn't need to factor p - 1 at runtime -- that hard work was done offline by the author and baked into the DLL. Dennis Schwarz noted these were \"likely something Fermat number related,\" and he was onto something: the primes are chosen so that their totient has a smooth factorization, making generator-finding tractable.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe number of domains to generate comes directly from q - 1. For the first config, this works out to 22 domains; for the second, 28 -- totaling 50 domains per weekly rotation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eFinding generators: A textbook primitive root search\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith p and the prime factorization of p - 1 in hand, Bedep needs a generator of the multiplicative group ((\\mathbb{Z}/p\\mathbb{Z})^*) -- or more precisely, of a specific subgroup of order q.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe \u003ccode\u003etransform8\u003c/code\u003e function does this in two stages. First, it runs a \u003cstrong\u003etrial-division prime sieve\u003c/strong\u003e: starting from 3, stepping by 2 (skipping evens), it tests each candidate against all previously found primes. It collects up to 37 small primes, then filters for those that actually divide q (the subgroup order). This gives the prime factorization of q.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThen \u003ccode\u003etransform3\u003c/code\u003e finds the \u003cstrong\u003eprimitive root\u003c/strong\u003e itself. This is the standard algorithm straight from a number theory textbook:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003edef find_primitive_root(p, prime_factors_of_order): \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;for g in range(2, p // 2): \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;is_generator = True \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;for q_i in prime_factors_of_order: \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;# If g^((p-1)/q_i) ≡ 1 (mod p), then g generates \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;# a proper subgroup, not the full group. Reject it. \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;if pow(g, (p - 1) // q_i, p) == 1: \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;is_generator = False \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;break \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;if is_generator: \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;return g \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;return None\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThe actual code in \u003ccode\u003etransform3\u003c/code\u003e is this logic rendered in C-style loop structure with manual index tracking, but the mathematics is identical. For each candidate g starting from 2, it checks:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e[ g^{(p-1)/q_i} \\not\\equiv 1 \\pmod{p} \\quad \\text{for all prime factors } q_i \\text{ of } p-1 ]\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf g passes all checks, it generates the full group. This is literally the algorithm from Section 11.1 of Shoup's \u003cem\u003eA Computational Introduction to Number Theory and Algebra\u003c/em\u003e, implemented in a malware DLL.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eWalking the cyclic group\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eNow comes the payoff. With a prime p, a generator g, and a subgroup order q, the \u003ccode\u003etransform7\u003c/code\u003e function sets up the DGA's iteration state:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003emodulus \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;= p \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;# field_c seed \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; = pow(g, random_e, p) \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;# field_4 -- starting element step \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; = pow(g2, random_f, q) \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; # field_8 -- step exponent\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThe starting position \u003ccode\u003eseed\u003c/code\u003e is g raised to a random exponent (derived from \u003ccode\u003erdtsc\u003c/code\u003e, the CPU timestamp counter). The step is similarly randomized from a second generator. Both are computed via modular exponentiation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThen, for each domain, the iteration is simply:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eseed = pow(seed, step, modulus) \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; # one step through the cyclic group domain = seed_to_domain(seed, currencies)\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eThat single line -- \u003ccode\u003epow(seed, step, modulus)\u003c/code\u003e -- is the entire DGA engine. Each call advances the state to the next element of the cyclic subgroup.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBecause the subgroup has order q, and step is coprime to q (guaranteed by the generator construction), this walk visits every non-identity element of the subgroup exactly once before cycling. The number of domains generated equals q - 1, which is precisely the number of non-identity elements. No collisions. No wasted iterations. No off-by-one hoping you hit enough domains.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe key insight:\u003c/strong\u003e the set of group elements visited is deterministic (fixed by p, g, and the exchange rates), but the \u003cem\u003eorder\u003c/em\u003e of visitation depends on the random exponents from \u003ccode\u003erdtsc\u003c/code\u003e. Every infected machine walks through the same set of q - 1 elements, generating the same q - 1 domain names, but potentially in a different order. Same destinations, different paths.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eflowchart TB \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;subgraph cyclic_group [\"Cyclic Subgroup of (Z/pZ)*\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;E1[\"g^1 mod p\"] --\u0026gt; E2[\"g^2 mod p\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;E2 --\u0026gt; E3[\"g^3 mod p\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;E3 --\u0026gt; E4[\"...\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;E4 --\u0026gt; Eq[\"g^(q-1) mod p\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;Eq --\u0026gt; E1 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;end \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;subgraph walk [\"DGA Walk (order depends on rdtsc)\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S1[\"seed_0\"] --\u0026gt;|\"pow(s, step, p)\"| S2[\"seed_1\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S2 --\u0026gt;|\"pow(s, step, p)\"| S3[\"seed_2\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S3 --\u0026gt;|\"pow(s, step, p)\"| S4[\"...\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S4 --\u0026gt;|\"pow(s, step, p)\"| SN[\"seed_(q-2)\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;end \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S1 -.-\u0026gt;|\"= g^e mod p\\n(random start)\"| cyclic_group \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S2 -.-\u0026gt;|maps to| E3 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;S3 -.-\u0026gt;|maps to| Eq\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eFrom Group Elements to Domain Names\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach group element is a 32-bit integer. Turning it into a domain name is the final step, handled by \u003ccode\u003etransform11\u003c/code\u003e. This is less mathematically elegant and more of a traditional mixing function, but it has a nice property: every character position in the domain uses a \u003cem\u003edifferent\u003c/em\u003e currency rate.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe algorithm cycles through the full list of parsed currencies (31 in the example above). For a domain of length 18, that means character 0 is influenced by USD, character 1 by HRK, character 2 by MXN, character 3 by GBP, and so on. Each character is computed by:\u003c/p\u003e\u003col\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e1b4d60fdb8fc1f153baf8a99855a0b35\"\u003eMultiplying the currency's 3-letter name (packed as a 32-bit int) and its 64-bit floating-point rate by large constants\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec05b47f97cd63ed0295b790fd5474c85\"\u003eXORing and shifting the result with the current seed and the days-since value\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e2f1e2c5d769b5e8978ca12f1d05cf791\"\u003eTaking the result modulo 26 (for positions 2+) or modulo 36 (for the last two positions, allowing digits)\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e589b38ae5aa632fba755388fc26bd71c\"\u003eAdding the ASCII offset for 'a'\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe domain length itself is derived from the seed XORed with a rate-dependent value, bounded between 12 and 18 characters. All domains use the \u003ccode\u003e.com\u003c/code\u003e TLD.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA trace from the PoC output shows the mixing in action:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eseed: 0xa13c9652 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;mixing in USD's rate \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;mixing in HRK's rate \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;mixing in MXN's rate \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;mixing in GBP's rate \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;... \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;mixing in ZAR's rate \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;domain: rrpohktjlscncqxvt3.com seed: 0x558af439 \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;mixing in USD's rate \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;... \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;domain: wjavcjhazzxyxotkbi.com\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eEach domain is a fingerprint of the entire exchange rate vector, filtered through one specific element of the cyclic group.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe Full Pipeline\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003ePutting it all together:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eflowchart LR \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;ECB[\"ECB XML\\n31+ currency rates\"] --\u0026gt; DateSelect[\"Select last\\nTuesday's rates\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;Earth[\"earthtools.org\\nUTC timestamp\"] --\u0026gt; DateSelect \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;DateSelect --\u0026gt; Mix[\"XOR/multiply\\nrates + days_since\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;Mix --\u0026gt; TableLookup[\"Lookup table\\n→ prime p, order q\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;TableLookup --\u0026gt; GenFind[\"Sieve primes,\\nfind generator g\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;GenFind --\u0026gt; GroupSetup[\"seed = g^rand mod p\\nstep = g2^rand mod q\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;GroupSetup --\u0026gt; Walk[\"Iterate:\\nseed = seed^step mod p\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;Walk --\u0026gt; CharMix[\"Mix seed with\\ncurrency rates\"] \u0026nbsp; \u0026nbsp;CharMix --\u0026gt; Domains[\"22-28 domains\\nper config\"]\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eSeven configs were observed in the wild, each with its own embedded table, XOR constants, and currency count (36 or 48). Each malware variant embedded two configs that ran per rotation -- the first generating 22 domains, the second 28, yielding 50 domains total. Across the observed campaign, domains were registered 2-5 days ahead of use through \"Domain Context\" registrar with Regway nameservers, all resolving to infrastructure at 5.196.181.244 and 46.105.251.1.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy this matters\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCompare Bedep's approach with its contemporaries:\u003c/p\u003e\u003ctable class=\"table\"\u003e\u003cthead\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003cth\u003eFamily\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eDGA Technique\u003c/th\u003e\u003cth\u003eSeed\u003c/th\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/thead\u003e\u003ctbody\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eConficker.C\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eDate + simple arithmetic\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eCurrent date\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eGamaredon\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eNested character-range loops\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eHardcoded ranges\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eNecurs\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eCRC32 hash of date components\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eDate + constant\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003eMatsnu\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eDictionary word concatenation\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003eDays since epoch\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003ctr\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBedep\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCyclic group walk mod p\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003ctd\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eECB exchange rates\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/td\u003e\u003c/tr\u003e\u003c/tbody\u003e\u003c/table\u003e\u003cp\u003eEvery other DGA on this list can be pre-computed arbitrarily far into the future. Bedep cannot -- because Tuesday's exchange rates don't exist until Tuesday. You can sinkhole Conficker's domains for next year during your coffee break. For Bedep, you have to wait for the ECB to publish, then race the botmaster to register the domains.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe mathematical guarantee is real: the cyclic group structure ensures exactly q - 1 distinct domains per rotation with no collisions and no wasted iterations. The primitive root construction ensures the generator produces every element. The precomputed tables avoid expensive factorization at runtime. The exchange rate seed provides unpredictable entropy from a trusted, globally-consistent, publicly-verifiable source.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is not someone who copy-pasted a DGA from a forum. The author understood multiplicative groups modulo primes, knew how to find primitive roots, precomputed smooth-order primes offline, and wired the whole thing to the European Central Bank's daily publications. Whether they learned this from a university course, a textbook, or a cryptography library's source code, the result is unmistakable: this is applied algebra, deployed in production, at scale.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe original ASERT analysis is preserved on the \u003ca href=\"https://web.archive.org/web/20160311083109/http://www.arbornetworks.com:80/blog/asert/bedeps-dga-trading-foreign-exchange-for-malware-domains/\"\u003eWayback Machine\u003c/a\u003e. The proof-of-concept implementation was published on \u003ca href=\"https://github.com/arbor/bedep_dga\"\u003eASERT's GitHub\u003c/a\u003e. The sample analyzed here is \u003ccode\u003ee5e72baff4fab6ea6a1fcac467dc4351\u003c/code\u003e (MD5) /\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003ed0fb1b66b6e4da395892327be9f39adb4533e7759ace39f67bdde0bb1cdaef35\u003c/code\u003e (SHA256).\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003eCredits: The DGA was originally reversed by Dennis Schwarz at Arbor Networks / ASERT. ","authors":[{"socialLinks":[],"bio":"A group of elite researchers who like to stay under the radar.","displayName":"Threat Research Team","title":"Threat Research Team","picture":{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_large/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=Y7ujGs-y 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_large_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=zua2szJm 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":425,"height":425},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_small/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=qctXxwnL 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_small_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/AdobeStock_618554154-2-2048x886.jpeg.webp?h=d91d38c4\u0026itok=KgS0xjxS
